## Appendix A. Glossary

Every term of art the plan uses, one line each, with the section that defines it. The defining section governs
where this appendix abbreviates. Terms are in alphabetical order; a term in two senses has two rows. Identifier
prefixes (BET, GOAL, NS, C, T, A, DC, GATE, KILL, INT, INV, PO, OPEN, ADR, FD, WP, BD, GB, KA, H, N, FMT,
FROB-REF) are registered in §0.3 and appear here only where the thing they name needs a definition. Numbers
quoted in a row are DESIGN values of the cited section unless the row says otherwise.

| Term | Meaning here |
|---|---|
| Acceptance predicate | The Boolean predicate A on executions that a mission query must satisfy; written in the requirement language (C-17, C-30, §24.5) |
| Adjoint, coadjoint | Ad_g moves twists and Ad_g⁻ᵀ moves wrenches; the algebra coadjoint is ad*_ξ = ad_ξᵀ with no minus sign, so that μ̇ = ad*_ξ μ + w (§11.2, §13.3) |
| Admission gate | An `INT-*` test that a sibling foundation must pass at its pinned commit before any crate may depend on it (§33.6) |
| ADR | An architecture decision record `ADR-<SUBSYS>-nn`: a decision with its reason, stated in the chapter that owns it |
| Airlock | `frob-fsim`, the only crate that names FrankenSim's volatile types (§33.3) |
| Alias witness | See indistinguishability witness; the observation-alias form of §20.12 names two distant states that deliver the same readings |
| Amdahl ledger | The table of phase shares of an incumbent design loop from which every ceiling of the thesis is computed (§2.2) |
| Anchored position | An integer cell index plus an `f32` offset in a cell of side 8 m, the offset centred in [−4, 4) m so that a cell shift is exact (§11.1, §35.8) |
| Annex | A separate workspace or project outside the core's lockfile and rules, kept apart by the unsafe and foreign-interface policy and, where it is Rust, built on the same toolchain T: `spark/`, `derive/`, `bindings/`, `viewer/`, `lean/` (§34.1). `surface/frob-mcp` is a member of the core workspace, not an annex |
| Answer | What a mission asks for: a design and controller that pass a frozen battery, a family answer, a correct withdrawal or confirmation after an amendment, or a truthful `unknown` with its witness (§2.1) |
| Anytime-valid study | A study whose inference is an e-process or a confidence sequence, valid at every stopping time (§24.12) |
| Archetype (design) | One of the eighteen design templates that `design.templates` lists (§14.10) |
| Archetype (interior) | One of the seven interior families with a generator, a structural proxy and a hazard list (§19.7) |
| Arena | The subsystem that owns tasks, requirements, fixtures, metrics and the qualification lane (§24) |
| Arena (device) | The preallocated structure-of-arrays slabs that hold every per-world quantity on a device; no allocation after environment creation (§21.10) |
| Artifact graph | The record that links every claim to the evidence, runs and decision rules that produced it (§29.13) |
| Artifact guard | The three checks a failure or a witness must survive before Nemesis counts it: replay one tier up, replay under numerical variants, and the energy audit (§26.6) |
| Assumption lattice | Assumption sets ordered by strength, on which every certificate records a polarity (C-18, §28.13) |
| Atelier | The subsystem that owns the studio loop: analyses, comparison, lineage, optimization, the ladder, the cascade and the decision cores (§28) |
| Audit lane | The share of a rung's spend given to designs drawn by a keyed stream before any score exists and evaluated at the higher rung at fixed n; the only source of calibration (§28.9) |
| Augur | The subsystem that owns mission queries and inverse design (§25) |
| Authority model | The declared law by which forces or actuators can be commanded, for example independent normal forces or equal passive springs; a field of every stance result (C-32, §25) |
| Autopsy | The subsystem that owns counterfactual debugging by forks and interventions (§27) |
| Background referee | The CPU referee re-running a keyed sample of fast-tier world-seconds and one-step defects, with a reported coverage figure (§16.11) |
| Base interval | A piece [t0, t1) of rational time cut by the physics grid and the clock boundaries; the unit a transaction commits (§16.5) |
| Baseline controller | The estimator and controller synthesized from the model for every design, with no training (§22.3, §22.4) |
| Batch-composition invariance | The property that a world's trajectory does not depend on batch size, slot, co-tenant worlds, bucket capacity or shard count (§35.5) |
| Battery (evaluation) | A frozen, versioned set of scenarios against which a design is accepted (§24.9) |
| Bead | A unit of delivery work with a `BD-nnn` identifier and a structured log (§45, §29.14) |
| Bet | A hypothesis the plan depends on, stated with its falsifier and a potency control (§4) |
| Brick pool | The shared pool of distance-field bricks from which the scene variants resident on a device are assembled (§19.15) |
| Brownout | A bus power demand above E²/(4·R), for which no operating point exists; recorded as an event (§15.10) |
| Budget stop | A stop on budget that publishes nothing partial as a result, except that a mission query returns its certified cells and the unresolved remainder (§25, §32.9) |
| Build packet | The bounded manufacturing export `.fbuild`: geometry, tolerances, datums and fiducials, load envelope and evidence, with `fabrication_released` always false (§31.12) |
| Campaign (design) | A declared sequence of evaluations toward one design decision, with a budget and a report (§28) |
| Campaign (qualification) | A set of confirmation requests that share a claim family, a sealed world family and a total error budget α (§24.13) |
| Campaign quotienting | Evaluating one canonical representative per Γ_obs orbit and per mission-observable class (C-22, §17.11) |
| Canonical form | A design written as its dimensionless groups after division by its own scales m₀, ℓ₀ and t₀ (C-21) |
| Capability scope | A named permission attached to a session, such as `qual:admin` or `qual:run` (§32.13, §37.5) |
| Capacity bias | The bias introduced when contact-capacity overflow removes worlds from one arm of a comparison; such a verdict is labelled `capacity-biased` (§18.11) |
| Card | Immutable, content-addressed data about a real or generic part, with provenance per field: `.fcard` kinds actuator, battery, transmission, interface, material, optical, sensor, payload (§15.3) |
| Cascade | The six-stage co-design sequence: deduplicate and check, reuse and synthesize, screen, race, rank robustness, qualify (§28.11) |
| Causal policy | A map from the history of arrived packets to commands; the policy class Π_Y of a mission query (C-19, §20.15) |
| Cayley map | The rational retraction cay(x) = (I − x/2)⁻¹(I + x/2) (C-05) |
| Ceiling | The most a mechanism could gain, stated before any speed test (D-10) |
| Cell (clearance) | A cube of the (x, y, ψ) grid of a clearance complex, with two one-sided clearances (§19.11) |
| Cell (mission) | A box of a design family's parameter space that carries one verdict and one certificate (§25.3) |
| Cell (reality) | The scope of a Reality result: physical units, controller and firmware, surface, task family and envelope of conditions (§30.5) |
| Certificate bundle | The portable `.frobcert` file that carries statements, proof objects and receipts for `frob-verify` (§12.4) |
| Certified | The evidence class of an instance-level statement accepted by a checker with an offline-verifiable certificate (§12.2) |
| Chronicle | The subsystem that owns telemetry, replay, forks, ledgers and the artifact graph (§29) |
| Chunk | The unit of published telemetry, written once and addressed by key (§29.4) |
| Claim | One of seven kinds that are never conflated: Proof, Known answer, Parity, Fidelity, Design comparison, Reality, Performance (§8.1) |
| Claim product | The type of a result that rests on two claims; its class is the weakest input's and nothing converts it into a proof (§8.2, §12.2) |
| Clearance complex | The pair of cubical complexes 𝒞_in ⊆ 𝒞_free ⊆ 𝒞_out of a planar base in one fixture mode, stored as `.fclear` (C-33, §19.11) |
| Clock contract | A task's declaration of rational periods, phases, the seven instants and the rule for intra-step events (§20.4) |
| Cohort | A set of model slots and worlds running under one step plan (§17.4) |
| Cold run, warm campaign | A cold run includes authoring the environment and the design domain; a warm campaign runs a declared sequence of amendments with reusable inputs (§2.1) |
| Commit fence | The rule that readers see only state at a committed `commit_epoch`; a candidate interval becomes visible in one step (§16.5) |
| Committed frontier | The contiguous prefix of published chunks to which a paginated query is pinned (§29.4, §32.8) |
| Compute envelope | An owner-approved resource class E0 to E3 for a run; E0 is 16 GiB, 8 cores, one hour, one device (§36.14) |
| Confirmation world | A world from a sealed cluster drawn fresh for one qualification request and unreadable by discovery until the campaign closes (§24.13) |
| Conformal margin | q̂, the ⌈(n + 1)·(1 − α)⌉-th smallest audit residual between two rungs or tiers (C-26, §28.9) |
| Construction | A mathematical object of the plan with its test, honest limits, tier and, for [F] and [M], pilot and ceiling: C-01 to C-40 (§10) |
| Contact capacity | The fixed maximum number of contacts per world in a shape class (§16.6, §18.11) |
| Contact law | A named law of the contact solve: CL-1 compliant default, CL-2 Hunt–Crossley, CL-M import mapping, CL-H hard contact (§18.5) |
| Controller arm | The declaration, in a comparison of designs, of whether the controller is shared, retuned with equal resources, or inherited (§24.12) |
| Convergence gate | A `GATE-*` row: one claim, one test, one numeric threshold and one milestone (§40) |
| Cortex | The subsystem that owns the gym, learning, family policies and inheritance (§23) |
| Cosserat rod | A one-dimensional elastic body with a frame per cross-section; the [M] model of cables and cords inside a coupled island (§19.10) |
| Counterexample | The smallest hand-checkable case that breaks a plausible shortcut; a permanent negative control (D-9, Appendix N) |
| Coupled island | A free-surface fluid, cloth or rod solver that runs beside one world on the CPU referee, for finalists only and behind a flag (§19.10) |
| Crossover reuse count | The number of amendments after which one-time authoring has paid for itself in a warm campaign (§2.1) |
| D0 to D4 | Determinism classes: exact; bitwise across backends; bitwise per backend and independent of the batch; tolerance with decision agreement; unspecified (§35.1) |
| Decimation | d, the control period divided by the physics step h; an env-step is d world-steps (§36.2) |
| Decision core | One of eleven recurring decisions (DC-01 to DC-11), each with a compiled table, a loss model, proof obligations, a deterministic fallback and a budgeted mode (§28.19) |
| Decision-level equivalence | The test under class D3 that task decisions agree between two runs, not only that state errors are small (§35.4) |
| Defensive mixture | A proposal q = β·p + (1 − β)·q′ whose importance weights are at most 1/β (C-27, §26.9) |
| `Degraded`, `Faulted` | World states. `Degraded`: a world that exceeded a contact or row capacity or did not converge, marked with its reason and never silent. `Faulted`: a world whose transaction failed every numerical plan, with cause `numerical-fault` kept apart from physical failure (§16.6, §16.14) |
| Dependency firewall | The checks that keep sibling and third-party dependencies out of crates that may not have them (§33.7) |
| Derivative-validity label | One of nine labels a derivative carries: `smooth`, `saltation`, `discrete-step`, `fixed-mode`, `one-sided`, `generalized`, `smoothed`, `finite-difference`, `refused` (C-16, D-15) |
| Design | A typed term in the design language, stored as `.fbot` with schema `frob.design/1` (§14.3, §14.4) |
| Design corpus | About 200 designs (templates, parameter perturbations, designs with planted defects, planted winners and planted causes) with labels fixed before any analysis is tuned (§24.9) |
| Design family | A set of designs over typed holes and parameter charts on which a mission query or a family batch ranges (§25.3, §17) |
| Design-family bucket | A packing of a family's worlds in which constants shared by its members are stored once; the device form of a family lane (§21.11) |
| Determinism profile | A named set of pins a run declares: `reference-replay`, `device-replay`, `cross-backend`, or the labelled class-D4 `fast-training` (§35.2) |
| Device-second | One second of one device's wall-clock occupancy, including compilation, admission, transfer and synchronization (§36.2) |
| Differentiator | One of the thirteen statements N1 to N13 of what the project adds, worded as the audit permits (§1.6) |
| Disclosure block | The fields every Performance claim carries: hardware, versions, world count, step and decimation, solver settings, contacts, sensors, class, timings and memory (§36.7) |
| Discovery lane | Everything an agent may read while it designs: training scenes, development splits, batteries, the hard-world set, its own runs (§24.13) |
| Divergence ledger | Recorded CPU-versus-device differences per kernel (§21.13) |
| Docking boundary | The closed-form condition ε < (U + ½)/4 of the docking fixture, equality excluded (C-19, §20.16) |
| Docking fixture | A unit mass with hidden gain g ∈ {1, 2}, three ticks and one noisy velocity packet; the known answer for information timing (C-19, §20.16) |
| Doctrine rule | One of D-1 to D-19, the rules every construction obeys (§9) |
| Doubly robust tier correction | The estimator Z = m + A·(Y − m)/p that stays unbiased whatever the cheap prediction m is (C-25, §29.11) |
| Dual witness | A vector y that proves infeasibility of a relaxation: Mᵀy ≥ 0 and rᵀy < 0 (C-32) |
| E-process | A nonnegative supermartingale under the null, here the betting product Eₙ = ∏ (1 + λᵢ·d̃ᵢ) (C-15) |
| Edit | A typed change to a design that declares its correspondences and returns an invalidation set (§14.7) |
| Edit-to-first-step latency | The wall time from an accepted `design.edit` to the first batched step of the new design (§17.8) |
| Energy budget | The `.fenergy` inequality that bounds every source of energy that can reach an absorber by the absorber's minimum work (C-31, §22.11) |
| Energy ledger | The per-episode attribution of every joule to a store, a loss or an export, with the numerical residual on its own line (§29.9) |
| `energy-created` | The flag of an episode in which created energy exceeded 1% of the absolute work exchanged in any 0.5 s window on a fast tier, or 10⁻⁶ relative on the referee (§29.9; applied by §16.10) |
| Env-step | One control step of one environment: d world-steps (§16.1, §36.2) |
| Envelope (fidelity) | The frozen tolerance within which a faster tier must agree with a slower one for a result to be promoted (§16.8) |
| Envelope (output) | The common wrapper `frob.envelope/1` of every verb's output, with claims, reality status, budget and refusal fields (§32.4) |
| Established | The label of a result re-evaluated on a higher tier and found inside its envelope; names its adjudicator (§16.8) |
| Estimated | The evidence class of a number from a model or a statistical procedure, with its uncertainty (§12.2) |
| Evidence class | Proved, Certified, Tested or Estimated, ordered strongest to weakest (§12.2) |
| Exact-integer kernel class | Kernels in integer arithmetic for strict predicates, digests and histograms; class D0 on every backend (§21.8) |
| Exclusion | A cell removed from the outer set with a refutation of matching scope (C-17) |
| Explicit reset | The default gym semantics: an ended world keeps its final observation until the caller resets it (§23.3) |
| Exploitation detector | A re-evaluation under a perturbation that should not matter, which flags a policy that depends on it (§23.16) |
| Facility co-design | Designing the robot, the building amendments and the handoff between robots in one query [M] (§25.17) |
| Family certificate | `Certified` evidence about a set of designs: a cell of the inner set or a cell removed from the outer set (§12.2) |
| Family lane | Worlds that share a tree, a sensor graph and, for a stretch of time, a contact mode, so that structure is constant across them (§17.6) |
| Family policy | One set of weights that acts on the tokens of any design of one morphology class (C-23, §23.11) |
| Feasibility witness | A face-connected path of inner cells from start to goal in a clearance complex (§19.11) |
| Fidelity ledger | Recorded differences between a faster tier and a slower one, per stratum (§16.8) |
| Film-friction law | The contact-level law in which friction on a wetted patch depends on dwell time, load and pad size (§18.8) |
| Firmware export | Export of a fixed-point controller with its Q formats and test vectors against caller-supplied interfaces [M] (§31.13) |
| First-divergence search | The search over the chained digests of two runs for the first tick and stage at which they differ (§27.10) |
| Five Explicits | Units, seeds, budgets, versions and capabilities are never implicit at the agent surface (§32.2) |
| Fixed-n exact study | A study with an exact interval or test at a sample size fixed in advance, admitted only with a reachability pre-analysis (§24.12) |
| Fixture | A small registered task with a closed-form or enumerated answer, for example `fixture:cart-sill-dock` (§24.10, §24.11) |
| Fleet | The two machines under one scheduler: the M5 Ultra holds the world bank, proofs, replay and large-memory jobs, the two RTX 4090 train, and only seeds, weights and receipts cross (§21.17) |
| Flip rate | ρ₀, the fraction of placebo forks whose verdict differs from the parent's (§27.5) |
| Floquet multiplier | An eigenvalue of the derivative of the return map of a periodic gait (C-16) |
| Fork | A child run started from a snapshot, with its own world address and fresh keyed streams (§27.3, §29.8) |
| Fork classification | The class the service assigns a requested change before restoring state: continuation, exogenous, contact-affecting, information, or structural (§27.4) |
| Foundation | A sibling project with an `FD-*` row: posture, pin, admission gate and fallback (§33.4) |
| Foundry | The subsystem that owns structure, manufacture and lifecycle (§31) |
| `frob`, `frobd`, `frob-mcp`, `frob-verify`, `frob-tui` | The CLI, the daemon, the MCP server, the offline certificate auditor and the terminal dashboard (§32, §12.4) |
| Gait orbit | A periodic gait computed as a fixed point of a return map, with its multipliers (C-16, §22.9) |
| Gauge | Directions that are unobservable by construction, such as global position and yaw under proprioception and gravity (C-20) |
| Gauntlet | The verification program, with tiers G0 laws, G1 known answers, G2 parity, G3 metamorphic, G4 chaos, G5 determinism (§38) |
| Generator | A keyed sampler of designs within a morphology class, identified by a `GeneratorId` (§17.10) |
| Ground | The subsystem that owns calibration against reality (§30) |
| Growth vector | The dimensions of the flag of a distribution under brackets; it decides controllability and the cost of small motions (C-07) |
| Guarded single precision | An `f32` predicate with a proved error bound that falls back to exact arithmetic inside the bound (§13.7, §21.8) |
| Habitat | The subsystem that owns scenes, living worlds, clearance and retrofits (§19) |
| Hard-contact law | CL-H: Signorini complementarity with Coulomb friction on the cone; the referee's specification and an optional batch law (§18.5) |
| Hard-world set | The versioned set of minimal counterexample worlds per task family and robot class; discovery data, never a confirmation sample (§26.8) |
| Hardware fingerprint | The digest of host, device, driver, runtime, compiler and probe results to which caches and receipts are bound (§21.16) |
| Hazard list | The registry, per interior archetype, of hazards with dimensions and provenance (§19.8) |
| Hervé class | One of the twelve conjugacy classes of connected subgroups of SE(3) (C-01) |
| Hypothesis verdict | The recorded state of one of the planning hypotheses H-01 to H-24: supported, narrowed or refuted (§0.8) |
| Idempotency key | The caller's key that binds a mutating request to exactly one effect (§32.7) |
| Identity | A content hash such as `DesignId`, `ModelId`, `SceneId`, `KernelId`, `PolicyId`, `EpisodeId`; it names bytes and carries no verdict (§11.1, D-11) |
| Impossibility cut | Disconnection of start and goal in the outer complex at a margin, with the separating cells named (§19.11) |
| Independently accepted | Accepted by a checker that is not the proposing system: a battery in a neutral evaluator, `frob-verify`, a neutral adjudicator (§2.1) |
| Indistinguishability witness | Two admitted worlds and a deadline such that every admitted command prefix yields identical packet histories while the successful continuations are disjoint (C-19) |
| Inheritance | Initializing the policy of an edited design from its parent by the transport of the edit (C-23, §23.12) |
| Inner and outer complex | 𝒞_in contains a cell only if every pose in it is free; 𝒞_out omits a cell only if every pose in it collides (C-33) |
| Inner and outer design sets | I and O with I ⊆ F ⊆ O: every cell of I has a witness, every cell outside O a refutation (C-17) |
| Inner and outer enclosure | Sets contained in, and containing, the occupied set of a body under the stated field and registration errors (§18.3, §19.5) |
| Instants (seven) | Sample, exposure, timestamp, arrival, decision, command, actuation, with a fixed boundary ordering (§11.8) |
| Intervention | A typed change do(X := x) to one declared mechanism, recorded as a model difference (C-29, §27.5) |
| Job handle | What a long verb returns when it does not finish inside `sync_wait_ms` (§32.10) |
| Joint-level rows | Stiction, backlash and hard stops written as rows of the same solve as contact (C-37, §18.7) |
| Kernel | A manifest, its arithmetic and its sources in Rust and the shared dialect (§21.5) |
| Kernel class | `float`, `exact`, `guarded` or `stat` (§21.8) |
| Kernel IR | The typed intermediate form `frob.kir/1` from which kernel sources are emitted and on which dimension checks and rewrite classes are decided (§21.7) |
| Kernel reflection | The route by which an instance certificate is promoted by evaluating a reflected checker inside a proof kernel (§12.4) |
| Kill gate | A `KILL-*` row: a trigger written in advance and the fallback that replaces the mechanism (§40) |
| Kinesis | The subsystem that owns dynamics on three tiers (§16) |
| Known answer | An analytic or enumerated truth a mechanism must reproduce; numbered `KA-nn` (§38, Appendix M) |
| Label count | The least number of calibration labels with which one controller serves all admitted variants by a deadline (C-19) |
| Ladder | The six evaluation rungs R0 static, R1 shared policy, R2 fine-tuned, R3 specialist, R4 referee, R5 rare failure (§28.9) |
| Layer rule | One of L-1 to L-12, the dependency rules between crate layers (§34.3) |
| Lease | An epoch-fenced right of the host to read or write a device slab (§21.10) |
| Legality set | The typed world edits, with bounds from cited norms, that an adversarial search may apply (§26.3) |
| Lemma | The subsystem that owns proofs, certificates and the offline auditor (§12) |
| Lifecycle compression | Advancing slow wear and ageing states over many hours of duty without simulating every step [M] (§31.14) |
| Lineage | The graph of designs linked by typed edits, with diff, blame, bisect and merge (§28.7) |
| Link token | The per-link input of a family policy: the link with its parent joint, in dimensionless features (C-23) |
| Living world | A scene whose state the robot changes: films, proxies, slosh and cords, admitted in three tiers (§19.10) |
| Load case | One instant of one episode from which structural loads are taken; peaks from different instants are never assembled (§31.6) |
| `lockstep` | The step profile of the throughput tiers: fixed step, fixed iteration count, convex compliant solve (§16.3, §16.6) |
| Marrow | The subsystem that owns the algebraic core: Lie types, groups, exact predicates (§13) |
| Mass-support bound | ‖c − o‖² + tr(I_C)/(2m) ≤ ρ² for a body whose mass lies in a ball of centre o and radius ρ (§11.5) |
| Mechanical group | Γ_mech, the automorphisms of the labelled kinematic tree realized by an isometry that preserves every mechanical parameter (C-09, §13.4) |
| Memo graph | The store of results under complete dependency keys, checked by shadow recomputation (§28.14) |
| Metamorphic test | A relation that must hold between two runs without knowing either answer, such as equivariance under a symmetry or exact similarity scaling (§38) |
| Minimal counterexample world | A failing world reduced to a 1-minimal set of typed edits on a passing world, stored as `.fmcw` with a solvability witness (C-28, §26.7) |
| Mission query | A design domain, a world set, an observation channel, a causal policy class and an acceptance predicate with their quantifiers, stored as `.fmission` (C-17, §25) |
| Mission-observable projection | A digest of the compiled model restricted to what the mission's equations read; equal digests are evaluated once (C-22, §17.11) |
| Mixed-topology batch | One batch whose worlds run robots with different kinematic trees (§17) |
| Model variant | A per-world model with its own tree, joint classes and actuator count inside the padded capacities of a shape class (§17.3) |
| `model-conditional` | The label of a ranking that reverses between contact laws or other model forms (§16.9) |
| Model-form rank check | The comparison of design rankings under two contact laws or stiffness variants (§16.9) |
| Module | A reusable sub-assembly `.fmodule` with a port contract and an invalidation contract (§14.6) |
| Moonshot cap | At most four [M] items are active at once; the rest wait in the queue of Appendix C (D-6) |
| Morphology class | A set of designs with one kind of body plan, for example quadrupeds, within which a family policy is trained and rank agreement is measured (§23.11, §28.10) |
| Negative control | A planted defect or deliberately invalid input that a gate must catch (§38) |
| Nemesis | The subsystem that owns adversarial worlds and rare-failure estimation (§26) |
| Neutral adjudicator | The party outside both arms of the ten-fold test that releases receipts only after both arms close (§2.1, §39) |
| No-authorization flags | `execution_authorized`, `hardware_safety_signoff`, `sim_to_real_certified` and, on build packets, `fabrication_released`: always false, with no setter (§12.2, §37.9) |
| Noninterference | Five properties: presentation, hidden truth, unused sensors and batch composition cannot change physics or delivered observations, and optics and contact cards cannot change each other's outputs (§20.17) |
| North-Star scenario | One of NS-01 to NS-24, an end-to-end session the plan must make possible (§7) |
| Null fork | The empty intervention; it must reproduce the parent bit for bit (§27.5) |
| Observability Gramian | W = Σ Φᵀ·Hᵀ·R⁻¹·H·Φ over a window, reported by its smallest singular value off the gauge (C-20, §20.11) |
| Observation-preserving subgroup | Γ_obs ≤ Γ_mech: the elements that also preserve sensors, payload, task goal, gravity and declared terrain structure (C-09, §13.4, §20.13) |
| One-step defect | The difference after one control interval between a device world and the CPU advanced from the same committed state (§16.11) |
| Optical volume | A box or ellipsoid with an extinction coefficient and a backscatter albedo, for steam, dust and glare (§19.3) |
| Packet queue | The pending sensor and command packets of a world; part of every checkpoint and restore set (§11.7, §16.5, §20.4) |
| Padding | Capacity of a shape class above a world's own size, masked so that it changes no result (§17.5) |
| Paired lower bound | The one-sided 95% lower confidence bound of a paired ratio; the only form in which a performance ratio is admitted (§36.6) |
| Parameter variant | A per-world set of continuous parameters within one tree (§17.3) |
| Partial ten-fold gate | The M3 measurement of the ratio on legged and wheeled briefs, recorded whatever its value, with a written rule for each outcome (GATE-TENFOLD-06; §39.12, BET-13). The tournament at M7 is GATE-TENFOLD-07 |
| Patch reduction | The documented replacement of the samples of a small contact patch by one contact, with its qualification fixture (§18.11) |
| Per-world mutable state | Scene state that a world may change, such as film thickness or a moved proxy, allocated from fixed-capacity pools and held in every snapshot (§19.3) |
| Phase accumulator | The integer counter by which a process with a rational period fires (§11.7) |
| Phase ledger | The measured breakdown of the wall time of a design loop by phase, device and agent turn, taken on both arms (§36.15, §39) |
| Phylum | The subsystem that owns families of bodies in one batch (§17) |
| Pilot | A paper-scale experiment with a written falsifier that must run before a Frontier or Moonshot work package is funded (D-10) |
| Placebo fork | A fork whose change no physical cause could depend on, used to measure how often chaos alone flips a verdict (§27.5) |
| Placement receipt | The record of which device each stage was requested on and where it actually ran (§21.12) |
| Planted winner | A known-good design inserted into a screened population; losing it voids the screen (§28.12) |
| Polarity | The direction in which a certificate survives an amendment of the world set, the policy class or the predicate (C-18) |
| Port | A typed interface of a component: mechanical, shaft, electrical, thermal or signal (C-12) |
| Posture | How a foundation is used: Consume, Subprocess, Lift, Oracle or Pattern (§33.1) |
| Potency control | A planted effect of known size that a bet's test must detect before a null result counts (§4) |
| Power-of-two similarity test | Scaling every dimensional input by powers of two according to its dimension and requiring the run to reproduce in every bit (C-21, §17.11) |
| Pre-analysis | For a fixed-n study, the list of verdicts reachable at that n and the power at declared alternatives (§24.12) |
| Precision profile | `default` or `stiff`, the declared arithmetic of the contact solve (§21.14) |
| Preflight | The eight stages of `design check` (§14.11) |
| Probability of necessity | PN(c) = P(no failure under do(¬c) given failure), estimated over failing worlds (C-29) |
| Probe | A physical experiment chosen so that mechanisms implying different designs give disjoint readings (C-39, §25.14) |
| Promotion | Re-evaluating a fast-tier result on a higher tier before it is called established (§16.8) |
| Proof kind | An entry of the closed registry of certificate forms a bundle may contain (§12.4) |
| Proved | The evidence class of a general statement accepted by a Lean kernel (§12.2) |
| `provisional` | The label of a fast-tier result that has not been promoted (§16.8) |
| Pseudo-inertia | The 4×4 matrix [[Σ, h], [hᵀ, m]] equivalent to the ten inertial parameters; consistent exactly when positive definite (C-03, §11.5) |
| Qualification lane | The blinded confirmation service: pre-committed requests, fresh sealed clusters and the error budget α_i = α/[i·(i + 1)] (§24.13) |
| Quality-diversity archive | A map of different good designs by behaviour cell, in place of one optimum (§28.8, §17.12) |
| Rank agreement | Agreement of the order of designs between two rungs or tiers: Kendall τ, a top-weighted τ and top-k recall with intervals (C-26, §16.9, §28.10) |
| Rational clock | A process whose period is an exact rational number of seconds, advanced by a phase accumulator (§11.7) |
| Reality band | The conformal interval [ŷ − q̂, ŷ + q̂] reported for a simulated metric inside a calibrated cell (§30.12) |
| Reality status | `no-claim`, `synthetic`, `published-anchor`, `candidate`, `calibrated` or `retired` (§30.2) |
| Referee | The most trusted tier, built on FrankenSim; not ground truth (§16.3) |
| Referee coverage | The share of fast-tier world-seconds, and of standard-battery scenarios, that the referee has re-run (§16.11) |
| Refusal | A structured error `FROB-REF-nnn` that names the rule a request violates (Appendix O) |
| Region certificate | An outward-rounded enclosure of one constraint on a box of at most about six design parameters (C-32, §25.6) |
| Registered prediction | A forecast an agent files before a run, scored afterwards with a proper scoring rule (§28.17) |
| Replication kit | The public bundle of data, commands and expected outputs with which a third party repeats a Ground result (§30.18) |
| Requirement | A signal-temporal-logic formula over unit-normalized predicates (C-30, §24.5) |
| Rescue rate | The fraction of replicates of an intervention in which the requirement is not violated (§27.6) |
| Restricted mean time | RMT = E[min(T, H)] at a common deadline H; an unresolved mission costs H (§2.1) |
| Retraction | A map from the Lie algebra to the group used to advance state (C-05) |
| Retrofit | A priced, typed amendment to the building, drawn from a catalog and compared with design changes in one budget (§19.12) |
| Return map | The map P of a Poincaré section to itself whose fixed point is a periodic gait (C-16) |
| Reuse grade | The licence under which a stored verdict skips a recheck: 1 identity, 2 dependency, 3 implication, 4 none (§28.13) |
| Revalidation | Re-deciding stored verdicts after an amendment by one engine behind `result.revalidate` and `mission.revalidate` (§28.13, §25.8) |
| Rewrite class | Class E rewrites change no bit under round-to-nearest with contraction off; class R rewrites are equal over the reals only and need an error bound and a new kernel identity (C-14, §28.19) |
| Rigid proxy | A body without internal joints that stands for a soft object: a rug as a free slab, a cord as a ridge or rod (§19.10) |
| Robustness | ρ = ψ/s, the margin of a requirement atom divided by its declared scale (C-30) |
| Rotor port | A thrust-producing actuator port for flight, admitted as [M] behind a flag (§15.4) |
| Rung | One level of the ladder of evaluation, allowed to decide only what its evidence supports (§28.9) |
| Saltation matrix | Ξ_e, the first-order map of perturbations across an isolated transverse event (C-16) |
| Sampling controller | A sampling-based predictive controller that needs no training; a screening scorer and fallback that never stands in for a trained policy as acceptance evidence. Its own episodes are accepted only where it is the delivered controller (§22.8) |
| Scene pack | A compiled scene `.fscene`: manifest, distance bricks, materials, fixtures, optical volumes and hazards (§19.3) |
| Scene split | The mechanical separation of training, development and held-out scenes, with building-level holdouts (§19.14) |
| Schedule class | An ordering of timing events modulo commutation of independent events; a race is explored once per class (C-19) |
| Scorecard | A design's metrics beside those of the reference robot of its class (§24.8) |
| `screening` | The label of a score from a rung that may discard and may not establish, such as a sampling controller or a gait orbit (§22.8, §22.9) |
| Sealed suite | The missions of the ten-fold test, fixed and sealed before either arm sees them (§39) |
| Segment mode | Re-simulation of one-second segments from fast-tier snapshots on a higher tier; it estimates a different quantity and says so (§16.8) |
| Sensorium | The subsystem that owns sensors, clocks and observability (§20) |
| Separating world | The admitted world nearest to nominal on which two closed loops receive different verdicts (C-18) |
| Settled-pose reset cache | Precomputed quasi-static start states per model, spawn, contact law and kernel version (§24.7) |
| Shadow recompute | Recomputing a sampled fraction of memo hits to detect an incomplete key (§28.14) |
| Shape class | The set of capacities one static step plan is built for, identified by a `ShapeClassId` (§17.4) |
| Shared dialect | The common subset of CUDA C++ and Metal Shading Language, with per-backend preludes (§21.6) |
| Shield | A runtime filter that projects a policy action onto barrier rows inside the actuator box, with a KKT check and a passive braking fallback (C-31, §22.12) |
| Similarity group | 𝕊 = (ℝ₊)³, changes of the units of mass, length and time, under which executions map to executions (C-21) |
| Sinew | The subsystem that owns actuators, transmissions and power (§15) |
| Size bucket | A shape class chosen so that robots of similar size share padded capacities (§17.5) |
| Skeleton | The subsystem that owns the design language, modules, archetypes and preflight (§14) |
| Skill | A behaviour stated at the task boundary, independent of the body, stored as `.fskill` (§22.10) |
| Skill transport | Carrying a skill to another body through its task interface, exactly where the interface permits and by a learned residual otherwise [M] (§22.10, §25.11) |
| Slot-level dispatch | The daemon's assignment of episodes to free world slots of a persistent environment; the unit of work is a world, not a job (§21.11, §32.11) |
| Snapshot | A full state of selected worlds sufficient for exact restart, including queues and warm starts (§29.8) |
| Solvability witness | Evidence that the candidate's own body can succeed in a failing world: `solvable-causal` or `plant-feasible` (§26.5) |
| Spark | The subsystem that owns GPU backends and the kernel toolchain (§21) |
| Specialist | A policy trained or inherited for one design alone; rung R3 (§28.9) |
| Specialization of survivors | Per-design kernel specialization, applied only to finalists and only if the profile justifies it (§21.18) |
| Splitting | Multilevel splitting on a distress score with fixed levels; an exploration method that issues `Estimated` rates and no bound (C-27, §26.9) |
| Stack lock | The file that pins every sibling's commit and records the one toolchain T, with a receipt of what was built (§33.5) |
| Stage signature | A kernel's declared read and write footprints per buffer and its aliasing rules, held in its manifest (§21.5) |
| Step plan | The static list of kernels, buffers and launches built at environment creation, identified by a `StepPlanId` (§21.11) |
| Step profile | `lockstep`, `strict` or `referee`; part of the step-plan identity every result carries (§16.3) |
| Straight-line program | Branch-free arithmetic with shared subexpressions; the form of derived kernel bodies (C-14) |
| Stratum | A block of design space, missions or contact modes for which a statistic or a verdict is reported separately |
| `strict` | The `batch-cpu` profile that runs the transactional, event-located step (§16.3) |
| Strongest composed rival | Leading simulators, an optimizer, analytic bounds, a verifier and the same agent, assembled by a competent engineer and frozen (§2.1, §39) |
| Structural proxy | The [S] form of an interior archetype: boxes and fixtures with the hazards' dimensions (§19.7) |
| Study | A registered plan to answer one comparative or absolute question, of type anytime-valid or fixed-n (§24.12) |
| Survivor set | Every design whose lower-rung score is within 2·q̂ of the k-th best lower-rung score (§28.9) |
| `sync_wait_ms` | The bounded synchronous wait a long tool takes before returning a job handle (§32.10) |
| Synthetic rehearsal | The run of the whole Ground procedure against data from a simulator standing in for reality; never promoted (§30.21) |
| Task | A `.ftask` file: terms, requirements, randomization, clocks and symmetry declarations (§24.4) |
| Telemetry level | T0 per-episode metrics, T1 events, T2 control-rate traces on sampled worlds, T3 restart snapshots (§29.5) |
| Teleport | Replaying a GPU world on the CPU from its address to find where tiers part (§29.8) |
| Ten-fold test | The comparison of restricted mean times to an independently accepted answer against the strongest composed rival on the sealed suite (§39) |
| Tested | The evidence class of a property held on a stated set of cases (§12.2) |
| Tether | The subsystem that owns the CLI, the daemon, the MCP server, the dashboard and the viewer (§32) |
| Theorem card | A statement the project wants, with its route and tier: T-01 to T-35 (§12.3) |
| Tie rule | If a short robust program over an incumbent engine ties, the plan says so and ships the layer (BET-14, §39) |
| Tier (maturity) | [S] Solid, [F] Frontier or [M] Moonshot, on every mechanism (§0.1) |
| Tier (physics) | `referee`, `batch-cpu`, `gpu-cuda` or `gpu-metal` (§11.1, §16.3) |
| Tier-correction ledger | The record of audited paired differences between tiers that feeds the doubly robust estimate (§29.11) |
| `tier-sensitive` | The label of a result that changes when it is re-run one tier up (§16.8, §23.16) |
| Toolchain T; toolchain train | T is the one Rust toolchain of the whole build graph, chosen at INT-TOOLCHAIN-01 and recorded in `franken-stack.lock` and `rust-toolchain.toml`. A toolchain train is the one coordinated change that moves T here and in every consumed sibling that needs it, followed by the admission and parity gates (§33.13) |
| Top-weighted τ | A Kendall rank correlation in which discordances near the top of the ranking count more (§16.9) |
| Traction | The subsystem that owns collision, contact, wheels, tracks and terrain (§18) |
| Transactional step | The referee and `strict` step: one transaction per base interval that commits a complete checked interval or leaves no trace (§16.5) |
| Transport | T_e, the initialization of a child's policy defined by the correspondence an edit declares (C-23) |
| Transversality number | D_t g + D_x g·f⁻, the rate at which a trajectory crosses a guard; near zero is grazing (C-16) |
| Trust ladder | The rule that fast-tier results are provisional until promoted, with detectors for exploitation (§16.8, §23.16) |
| Tube table | A table of mode-indexed cells with one safe action per cell, closed under resets, sensing error and disturbance (C-31) |
| `unknown` | A verdict: a solver failure, a timeout or an unenclosed branch never yields success or failure (D-13) |
| Unresolved cell | A cell of O ∖ I, reported with its competing explanations and the next test that would split it (C-17, §25.3) |
| Upstream work item | A change this program makes in a sibling repository, under that repository's own rules: a bead here with the label `upstream`, mirrored in the sibling's tracker, with an interim posture and a switch-over rule (§33.8, Appendix F) |
| Value of information | The expected reduction in decision loss from an experiment, by which experiments are ranked per unit cost (C-39, §28.16) |
| Verb | A `cluster.verb` entry of the one registry from which the CLI, the MCP tools and the documentation are generated (§32.6, Appendix J) |
| Verdict | The recorded result of applying a declared rule to evidence, with its class, scope and receipts (§28, §29.13) |
| Witness | An object that makes a claim checkable: a controller with its certificate for an inner cell, a path, a dual vector, a pair of worlds, a replayable trace |
| Witness migration | Re-detection of a contact's witness pair, with transfer of its tangential memory, when sustained contact moves across features (§16.5, §18.4) |
| Work package | A `WP-*` unit of funded scope with entry pilots and exit gates (§44) |
| World | One simulated instance of a scene variant, a robot and its random draws, named by a world address |
| World address | `(run_seed, world, episode)`; it determines every random draw a world sees (§11.1, §35.9) |
| World bank | The set of compiled scene packs resident on a device (§19.15) |
| World set | W, the worlds a mission query quantifies over, declared before any candidate is tested (C-17) |
| World-second | One second of simulated time of one world: 1/h world-steps (§36.2) |
| World-step | One physics step of one world (§16.1, §36.2) |
| Wreath product | K ≀ H, the group that acts when modules with their own symmetries sit in slots a hub permutes (C-22, §13.6) |

## Appendix B. Idea passes

This appendix records how the ideas in the plan were generated and winnowed, and where each surviving idea now
lives. It adds no design. Section numbers are those of this version. Row numbers in the tables are local to this
appendix and are not stable identifiers. Every ratio that a pass claimed for an idea was paper arithmetic and is a
HYPOTHESIS as proposed unless the text marks it FACT. Every threshold quoted from a gate or a kill gate is a TARGET
owned by the cited section; where this appendix and the gate register (§40) differ, the register governs. Ideas
that were refuted or deflated are named in §B.7 and treated, with the refuting calculation, in Appendix C. How the
passes were run and in what order is process provenance and is kept in Appendix K.

### B.1 The first pass: thirty ideas, five, fifteen

The first pass generated 30 ideas against the brief, thought each through (how it works, how an agent would
experience it, how it is built, how it fails), and winnowed them. The ranking criterion was *obviously accretive
and pragmatic for an agent designing a robot*, not mathematical interest. Every survivor is designed into the body
of the plan; this section says where. Where a later pass or the verdict register of §0.8 narrowed an idea, the
narrowed form is the one stated.

**The 30.** (1) a trust ladder with promotion and exploitation detectors; (2) a check-before-simulate static
analysis suite; (3) a synthesized baseline controller for every design; (4) single-source derived kernels on
three backends; (5) teleporting a GPU world to the CPU by address; (6) an energy ledger read from the port
structure; (7) anytime-valid design comparison on common random numbers; (8) morphology symmetry detection with
equivariant learning; (9) typed edits with lineage diff, blame and bisect; (10) scene splits enforced
mechanically; (11) actuator cards with provenance and a co-design closure check; (12) registered predictions with
per-agent calibration; (13) a quality-diversity archive of designs; (14) deterministic ray-kernel sensors and
stills with pixel inspection; (15) scorecards normalized against a reference robot per class; (16) a structural
margin loop from telemetry loads; (17) a gait atlas from symmetry with max-plus timing; (18) a maneuverability
index from Lie brackets; (19) a connection-curvature design oracle; (20) import loss reports with
physical-consistency repair; (21) a budget estimate before every expensive verb; (22) one verb registry that
generates the CLI, the MCP tools and the documentation; (23) a failure taxonomy with first-cause ordering;
(24) token-budgeted telemetry summaries; (25) self-contained HTML replays; (26) randomization derived from card
uncertainty; (27) certificates bound to kernel hashes; (28) racing optimizers that cancel losers; (29) a
behaviour-cloning warm start from the baseline controller; (30) a negative-control registry for every gate.

**The best five, best first.**

1. **The trust ladder (§16.8, §23.16; BD-104, BD-112).** Fast-tier results are provisional until a sample is
   re-run on a higher tier, and policies are re-tested under perturbations that should not matter. *Why it wins:*
   the most damaging failure of a learned-control simulator is a result that is an artifact of the simulator. It
   is also the cheapest to guard against, because the referee already exists in FrankenSim and the sample size is
   set by a sequential test. *Confidence:* high. The mechanism is simple, and FrankenSim already states the same
   pattern for its surrogates. *Since the first pass:* promotion compares task decisions as well as state errors
   (§35.4); finalists are re-ranked under other contact laws, cones, stiffnesses and steps, and a ranking that
   flips is typed `model-conditional` (§16.9, A-19); a paired tier correction reports a referee-level estimate
   (C-25, §29.11); a sampled background referee is [F] (§16.11).
2. **Check before you simulate (§14.11, §28.4; BD-053 to BD-061).** The analyses A-01 to A-26, of which those
   marked for `design check` run inside eight preflight stages in under two seconds (TARGET, GATE-ATEL-02 and
   GATE-ATEL-05), with repairs as typed edits. *Why it wins:* it moves the first feedback from the minutes of a
   training run (FACT, §1.1: about 410 s for a quadruped policy on one RTX 4090 in an incumbent) to seconds, it
   runs on a CPU with exact arithmetic where possible, it ships at M1 before any GPU work, and it is where the
   Lie theory visibly earns its place. *Confidence:* high for sizing, stability, symmetry and collision; medium
   for how well verdicts predict simulated failure, which BET-05 tests (GATE-ATEL-01, KILL-STATIC).
3. **A baseline controller for every design (§22.3, §22.4; BD-062 to BD-066, BD-113).** An estimator and a
   controller synthesized from the model. *Why it wins:* without it every design iteration contains a training
   run, and the loop of NS-06 is impossible. It also supplies demonstrations for warm starts (§23.6, §23.10).
   *Confidence:* medium-high for standard layouts, medium for novel ones; BET-06 and KILL-BASELINE handle the
   miss. *Since the first pass:* where the baseline is not synthesizable, a sampling controller gives a first
   behaviour as a screening rung (§22.8), and a family policy per morphology class is [F] (§23.11).
4. **Teleport, on single-source kernels (§21.6, §21.7, §29.8; BD-018 to BD-020, BD-102, BD-103).** Any GPU world
   can be re-run on the CPU from its address, and the first differing stage is named. *Why it wins:* a batched GPU
   simulator that cannot be debugged cannot be trusted, and counter-based addressing plus a CPU twin make this
   nearly free once designed in. *Confidence:* high for teleport; medium for the speed of derived arithmetic,
   which BET-01 tests and KILL-KIR covers. *Narrowed:* one kernel source lowered to several backends is prior art
   in incumbents (FACT, §1.1), so the differentiator claimed is the recorded cross-backend disagreement per
   kernel; and a teleported world is a bit-exact replay only where class D1 is attained, a tolerance replay
   otherwise (KILL-D1).
5. **The energy ledger (§15.11, §29.9; BD-046, BD-068).** Every joule attributed to a loss or a store, with the
   numerical residual on its own line. *Why it wins:* energy per metre is the figure of merit for a mobile robot,
   and knowing where the energy went tells an agent what to change. It falls out of the port structure instead of
   being bolted on. *Confidence:* high. *Since the first pass:* the same ledger drives the audit for energy
   created by the simulator (§16.10).

**The next ten.**

6. **Anytime-valid comparison on common random numbers** (§28.6; C-15; GB-06; BD-112): an agent can stop as soon
   as the answer is clear, and pairing by world address does more for sample size than the test itself. It is now
   one of two declared study types; the other is a fixed-n exact study with a pre-analysis of which verdicts are
   reachable at that n (§24.12).
7. **Symmetry as a computed property** (C-09; A-03; §13.4; BD-055, BD-117): the group is found, not declared; it
   gives augmentation, equivariant policies and a metamorphic test of the whole simulator per group element.
   *Narrowed:* a morphology has two groups. Closed-loop consumers (augmentation, equivariant policies of §23.13,
   symmetric controllers of §22.13) use only the observation-preserving subgroup, intersected with the
   stabilizers of the current payload, terrain and task (D-14). KILL-EQUIV keeps symmetry for augmentation and
   metamorphic tests if GATE-CORTEX-03 fails.
8. **Lineage with diff, blame and bisect** (§28.7; BD-116): version control for robots, so "which edit broke
   stairs" has an answer.
9. **Scene splits enforced mechanically** (§19.14; GATE-ARENA-02, GATE-HAB-14): a policy cannot be scored on a
   scene it trained on, by construction; a mirrored layout or a retrofit variant planted across a split is
   refused.
10. **Actuator cards and the closure check** (§15.3, A-10, A-11; GB-03; BD-043, BD-056): the mass spiral is
    decided before simulation, and no actuator constant is invented.
11. **Registered predictions** (§28.17; BD-116): a calibration record per agent, at almost no cost.
12. **A quality-diversity archive** (§28.8, §17.12; BD-116): a map of different good designs instead of one
    optimum.
13. **Deterministic ray-kernel sensing and stills** (§20.6, §20.9; BD-097, BD-098): depth, lidar and height
    scans share the physics determinism class, and a vision-capable agent can look and then ask about a pixel.
14. **Reference-robot scorecards** (§24.8; BD-111): every number has a familiar baseline beside it.
15. **A negative-control registry** (§38; GATE-GAUNT-01; BD-072): a gate that nothing can fail is not a gate.

**Ideas 16 to 29** are also in the plan, at lower priority or as Frontier items.

|---|---|---|---|

| 17 | Gait atlas from symmetry with max-plus timing | C-10; A-04; Reflex §22.9 | [F]. The atlas supplies clocks and seeds; an orbit ranks designs only if GATE-GAIT-03 passes (KILL-GAIT) |

| 28 | Racing optimizers that cancel losers | Atelier §28.8; C-15 | [S]. Expected saving: two to five times fewer evaluations when candidates are separated and none otherwise (HYPOTHESIS, §28.8) |

Of these, (19) remains the most likely to be cut. (16), which the first pass also expected to cut, was instead
taken up by four later passes and is now Foundry.

### B.2 The wider passes, and how to read them

After the first pass, three further rounds were run, each against a different draft of the plan. In each round
several independent passes generated about thirty ideas apiece under one question: what would make this program
an order of magnitude better than existing systems at its stated purpose. Each pass winnowed its own list and
then examined the others' lists: every idea received its strongest technical objection, and its proposer answered
or conceded. Audited reasoning passes checked claims against sibling source code, and the last round executed
small exact experiments on a CPU and on both GPU back ends. FACT (Appendix K): nine proposal passes produced about
225 raw entries, which merge into 112 distinct ideas.

Four rules govern how this material is read.

1. **Convergence is evidence about a problem, not about a mechanism.** An idea counts as converged when two or
   more of the nine passes proposed essentially the same mechanism without sight of each other; the count is the
   "Passes" column of §B.3. Several converged ideas were cut by every pass that proposed them, and several were
   refuted by calculation (§B.7).
2. **No claimed ratio is a result.** Outside the executed checks listed in Appendix K, every ratio in the passes
   was derived on paper. The plan carries such a ratio only as a HYPOTHESIS with its test, and states the ceiling
   of the benefit before any speed test (§36.5).
3. **Every idea has one of three dispositions.** Placed in the plan at a tier, with a gate and, for [F] and [M], a
   kill gate with its fallback (§B.3, §B.4); refuted or declined, with what survives (§B.7 and Appendix C); or not
   yet placed (§B.8).
4. **The cited section governs.** A row here is a pointer. The design, the thresholds and the fallback are in the
   section it names.

Tally of the 112 (FACT, by count against the chapters): 90 are placed in full or in a narrowed form, some rows
below merging several catalogued variants; 13 are refuted or declined outright; 9 are not yet placed.

### B.3 Ideas on which independent passes converged

"Passes" is the number of the nine proposal passes that proposed the mechanism. Rows are grouped by theme.

**Design language and reuse**

| # | Idea | Passes | Mechanism in one line | Where it lives | Tier | Gates, kill gates, bets |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Module substitution contracts | 4 | A robot is a wiring of typed open modules; a compiled module carries its port equations, an assume-and-guarantee summary and a dependency digest, so replacing a limb or an actuator invalidates only what depends on it | Skeleton §14.6 (`.fmodule`); C-12; Augur §25.21.1 | [S] modules and substitution; [F] reuse of certificates and analyses | GATE-SKEL-06; KILL-SKEL-SUBST. Negative control: two fingers on one bus, where a contract without time correlation prunes a feasible schedule |

| 88 | Ceiling before a speed test; reachability before a fixed-n study | Compute the ratio a test could show from its shared fixed work and refuse the test if that is below the claim; enumerate the verdicts a frozen rule can reach at its n | §36.5; Arena §24.12 (`eval.preanalyze`); Tether §32.6.1 | [S] | GATE-AUGUR-14; GATE-ARENA-05; GATE-ATEL-06 |

### B.5 Contested ideas

An idea is contested when independent examiners reached opposite conclusions about it, or when the passes that
proposed it disagreed about its form. Each row gives the strongest argument on each side and what the plan did.
The resolution is never a vote: it is a tier, a pre-declared test and a fallback.

| Idea | Strongest argument for | Strongest argument against | How the plan resolved it |
|---|---|---|---|
| The batch unit for many different robots (row 12) | Stepping many topologies in one launch removes a compile per design, and with few worlds per design it is the only way to fill a device | Homogeneity by morphology is not homogeneity by work: contact counts diverge inside a cohort; padding and bucket fragmentation eat the gain; a warm incumbent with per-family templates may win the early rounds | A four-arm head-to-head with outcomes declared in advance (§17.14): cohorts, nine per-family templates, an incumbent's mixed batch, and the leading incumbent design by design. KILL-PHYLUM makes the templates the engine if they win. The unit of measure is edit to first accepted result (§17.8) |
| Sampling-based control as the default controller (row 3) | It removes training from the design loop, and against per-design training it is the clearest large factor any pass found | A fixed-budget planner ranks bodies that are easy to plan for above better bodies; incumbents already run it; and it costs thousands of times as many model steps per simulated second as a policy rollout (DESIGN arithmetic on published FACTs, §22.8) | [F], as a screening rung of three episodes per design by default and as a demonstration source. It never stands in for a trained policy as the evaluator of record (FROB-REF-817, NONGOAL-14); the delivered-controller lane of §22.8 is the one exception. Its results carry `model-privileged` unless the planning model was drawn independently. GATE-REFLEX-06; KILL-MPC |
| Family policy across bodies (row 4) | One training is amortized over a class, which is where breadth per GPU-hour comes from | The pretraining budget is unowned; published transfer across morphologies is partial; novel hybrids are out of distribution; one proposer cut its own version and another demoted it after cross-examination | [F], per morphology class only (NONGOAL-10), above the exact skill layer of §22.10. GATE-CORTEX-11 and KILL-FAMILY decide it; a rung may discard designs only after rank agreement is measured (GATE-CORTEX-12; top-weighted Kendall τ thresholds 0.7 and 0.5) |
| Derivatives through the contact solve (row 16) | At a converged convex solve the adjoint is one back-substitution, and local margin sensitivities are a sound first consumer | The derivative is biased at make and break; the sibling's event-sensitivity code refuses simultaneous events, which a trot produces by design; a closed-loop design gradient also needs the controller's sensitivity | Not in the rigid core before M6 (NONGOAL-15). Fixed-mode derivatives on smooth kernels only, each with a validity label (D-8, C-16, §16.12). Return-map gradients under GATE-GAIT-02. Paired finite differences over short horizons are [F] under KILL-FDGRAD |
| Fused per-world step kernel (row 13) | Memory traffic is the lever, and once a sampling controller is in the loop stepping speed multiplies into every decision | The step is bound by latency and occupancy, not by operation count; one world's working set fills a block, so occupancy falls to a few percent of lanes; the proposed throughput was conceded | [M], behind a flag. KILL-MEGA is a one-week pilot with a paired lower bound of 2.0 on the whole step; the staged step plan is the design of record |
| Staged algebraic erasure as a throughput lever (row 60) | Doing the algebra at compile time makes the run-time step strictly cheaper | It restates a position the plan already held; the operation count falls by 18,000/9,600 = 1.875 on a step that is not bound by operation count; its falsifier bundled four treatments | Kept as a rule and not counted as a lever: checks at model compile time, no tags in kernels (C-02, C-14). Per-design specialization is for survivors only (KILL-SPECIALIZE) |
| Runtime shields from barrier conditions (row 25) | No model-level violation during exploration, and the same filter lowers to firmware | Three demonstrated defects: a "for every disturbance there is a control" condition admits a clairvoyant controller; an infimum over the smaller friction cone is optimistic for forces the environment chooses; a closed-form projection that ignores the box returns (1.25, 0.25) where the optimum is (1, 0.5) (FACT, hand-checkable; Appendix N) | [F], rewritten: one control for all disturbances and estimation errors; separate cones for controlled and environment forces; sort-and-clip for one row, a projected solve with a checked KKT certificate and a verified backup otherwise. Evidence class Tested. GATE-SHIELD-02, -03; KILL-SHIELD. The energy budget (row 23) comes first |
| A probability bound from assumption monitors (row 26) | Splitting a guarantee at assume-and-guarantee interfaces keeps the proof part honest about what it assumes | The bound cannot validate itself: a monitor cannot label its own silent breaches; distribution shift moves the miss rate; four independent feet with three states each give 27 strata and 162 transitions under the symmetry group, not 14 (FACT by count, C-16) | The typed product is [S] and is in every output envelope. The bound is reported only with breach labels drawn from hidden state independently of the monitor, with shifted worlds reported separately (GATE-SHIELD-03) |
| Observation-alias witness as a new capability (row 21) | It shows before any training that every policy on an interface must fail, and names the cheapest fix | Deriving the admissible action sets from a contact-rich plant is most of the work; given the same sets, an ordinary exact solver recovers every frontier (FACT, Appendix K) | Adopted as a checked witness, with no claim of a new algorithm. Structural and enumerated witnesses are [S]; searched witnesses are [F] and labelled experiment-specific. One false witness triggers KILL-ALIAS |
| Joint paired-outcome certification (row 70) | It closes whole parameter boxes of a design comparison at once | A conventional reachability tool handles the same product model, and one mislabelled draw voids the certificate | [F], on one contact transition. KILL-AUGUR-DOMINANCE falls back to paired replay; the rule that shared inputs are indexed by position and not by tick is kept in either case |
| Verdict reuse beyond a keyed cache (row 45) | Four passes proposed it; repeated work in a campaign is real | Executed check: a correctly keyed dependency graph skipped exactly the work the richer receipt skipped, and omitting one dependency inherited a false promotion (FACT, Appendix K) | The memo graph is [S] with a shadow recompute. Reuse by implication is [F] under KILL-REUSE and KILL-AUGUR-REUSE: one unsafe reuse removes the rule. Reuse by sensitivity margin is withdrawn (Appendix C) |
| Recomputing only what an intervention touches | A fork that recomputes only the descendants of its dirty frontier would make interventions cheap: a factor of 1/(0.05 + 0.019 + 0.01) = 12.7 if 95% of replay is addressable and 2% is dirty (HYPOTHESIS as proposed) | In a robot-centric scene the robot and its contact neighbourhood dominate compute and are always dirty; the dependency surface is large; full replay is simpler and exact | Not built. Forks copy full state by index, and a prefix is reused only when identical by construction (§29.8). A far-field lane is listed in §B.8 |
| Counterfactual branching: what a fork may claim (row 33) | Seven passes proposed it; it tells an agent which experiment to try next | Carrying state across a geometry, inertia or gearing edit is invalid; a mid-run rescue is conditional on a state the edited robot might never reach; coalition values can be negative and cannot establish necessity | [S], narrowed: diagnostic interventions fork from the snapshot; design-parameter edits re-run from the episode start (§27.8); forks are classified and the invalid ones refused (GATE-AUTOPSY-02); placebo forks and the flip rate bound instability (§27.5, §27.6); attributions are labelled and are never cited as established (FROB-REF-880) |
| Structure and manufacture inside the loop (row 37) | Distal link inertia changes dynamic rankings, the sibling structural crates exist, and a design that cannot be built is not a result | It adds scope and not decision speed; loads taken from compliant contact are artifacts of contact stiffness; sibling readiness was overstated and conceded | [F], declared an output-quality subsystem and not a speed lever (§31.2). Load cases are time-coherent and come from referee-tier telemetry (§31.6). Each stage has a kill gate with a simpler fallback |
| Catalog co-design by monotone bounds (rows 74, 75) | Discrete component selection is largely monotone, cheap to test on a CPU, and a sound cut on a partial assignment saves point checks multiplicatively | Monotonicity breaks under dynamics; a quasi-static filter can mis-rank; a shared resource is joint and time-dependent | [S] for catalog closure, [F] for antichains and cuts. KILL-SYNTH-CODESIGN fires on one falsely pruned design or on more than 20% of top designs mis-ranked; KILL-AUGUR-CUTS on one false cut |
| The estimator for cheap referee promotion (row 35) | Coupled estimation reuses an existing sibling estimator and gains (1 − ρ²); randomized audits stay unbiased when promotion targets are chosen after seeing fast-tier results | A unit-coefficient correction does not have the quoted variance; ρ falls on long contact episodes; inverse-propensity weights can exhaust the budget | Both, as one estimator: a fitted, frozen control-variate coefficient with a doubly robust audit correction on logged propensities (C-25). KILL-DR at ρ below 0.7 on 200 addresses falls back to the plain paired promotion |
| Sharing test cases across symmetry orbits (row 2) | An exact saving with no approximation | Interiors are asymmetric; floating point and unmirrored noise break exact invariance; the saving is on work one would not create | Orbits are taken only under the group that preserves robot, sensors, task and scene, and members have equal outcome distributions, not equal outcomes at equal addresses (§17.11). The known answers bound the lever: 86,185 orbits of 10⁶ labelings under the full dihedral group, 500,500 under a mirror alone. KILL-QUOTIENT below 5% of dispatches saved |
| Clearance complexes (row 18) | Fits, cannot fit or unresolved before any dynamics, reusable across a footprint family | It covers wheeled or rigidly folded bodies only; a conventional planning library could hold the same index; its share of design time is small and disputed | [F], two-sided with a reported band; a nonholonomic base without a steering primitive is labelled geometric only. KILL-FCLEAR falls back to a route-graph check labelled heuristic |
| Where the ten-fold comes from | Kernel throughput multiplies into every other mechanism | An infinitely fast environment step speeds training by at most about 7.7 times (1/(1 − 0.87)) on one incumbent stack and about 1.4 on another (FACT, §2.3) | Engine throughput is a parity requirement (KILL-PARITY); the ten-fold is sought in avoided trainings, family answers and fewer agent rounds (§2.4), and is measured, not argued (§39) |

Four disagreements did not move under cross-examination: whether kernel speed is undervalued, whether
closed-loop design gradients can work, whether randomized audits have a regime in which they beat coupled
estimation, and whether orbit sharing saves real work. The plan settles none of them by argument. Each has a
measurement in the rows above.

## Appendix C. EV matrix and the killed-idea register

This appendix has two jobs. C.1 scores every family of mechanism that was considered for the plan and records what
was decided about it: adopted at a tier, parked, deferred or rejected. C.2 holds the strong form of every idea that
was refuted or deflated, with the calculation or the evidence that did it and the narrower version that survives.
§0.8 requires that a strong form appear once in the plan, and this is the place. C.3 lists figures, in the
planning record and in other chapters, that do not follow exactly from their own formulas.

**Labels in this appendix.** A score is a DESIGN judgment. A figure quoted from a publication, from sibling code or
from a planning pilot that ran outside this repository is FACT with an entry in the source register (Appendix H),
and each is re-read from its primary source, or re-run here, before a gate cites it; that sentence is not repeated
in every row. Arithmetic on stated assumptions is marked "DESIGN arithmetic": a reader can redo it by hand, and it
is not a measurement. A range in a "survives" line, and a ceiling quoted in a decision column, is a HYPOTHESIS
about this system until the named gate records a measurement, and every threshold is a TARGET. A number inside a
"strong form" line is the proposal's own figure: it is quoted, not endorsed. Nothing here was measured on
FrankenRobotics code, because none exists.

### C.1 The EV matrix

Score = (Impact × Confidence) ÷ Effort, each from 1 to 5, printed to one decimal. Impact counts the effect on the
goal of the program, the time and compute to an independently accepted design decision (§2), and for evidence
mechanisms the effect on what may be trusted. Confidence is the judgment that the mechanism delivers that effect,
taken after the evidence of §0.8. Effort is engineering cost, with 5 the largest. Scores are judgments, recorded so
that they can be argued with.

Three rules govern the tables.

1. A family that scores below 2.0 is rejected, parked or deferred unless something else needs it. The one
   exception is a Moonshot: under GOAL-43 an [M] item may be admitted below 2.0, behind a feature flag and off the
   critical path, and it then waits in the queue of C.1.4 until its pilot is funded.
2. A row carried from version 0.2 keeps its scores unless a verdict of §0.8 changed them, and a changed score says
   so. Its "Where" column uses the final numbering.
3. Every construction C-16 to C-40 has a row, and so does every major mechanism that is not a construction. Where
   a construction has a Solid part and a Frontier part, the row sits in the table of its larger part and the
   decision names both.

Within each table the rows are sorted by score, highest first.

#### C.1.1 Adopted, Solid

These are the spine and the mechanisms next to it. A kill gate in the decision column names what the plan falls
back to; a row without one has no optional part.

| Family | Where | Impact | Confidence | Effort | Score | Decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Inertia on the positive-definite cone | C-03; §11.5, §13, §14.5 | 4 | 5 | 1 | 20.0 | Adopted [S]; Riemannian identification of inertial parameters is [F] in the calibration lane (§30.9) |
| Euler–Poincaré framing and the momentum map | C-04; §11.2, §16 | 4 | 5 | 1 | 20.0 | Adopted [S] |
| E-processes for paired comparison on common world addresses | C-15; §24.12, §28.6 | 4 | 5 | 1 | 20.0 | Adopted [S]; one of the two declared study types |

rejection rests on a calculation, the decision points to its entry in C.2. Methods that were considered for one
decision core and rejected are listed with their reasons in §28.19.4 and are scored here.

#### C.1.4 The Moonshot queue

D-6 caps the Moonshot budget: at most four [M] items are active at once, and an item is active from the funding of
its pilot until its expiry gate. The catalogue of admitted [M] items is longer than four, so the rest wait here.
Nothing in this table is on the critical path of §43, every item sits behind a feature flag, and none is funded
before its paper-scale pilot has run and its ceiling has been stated (D-10).

The order of entry into a free slot is a DESIGN default, and §43 governs where it differs. (1) An item whose
consumers land at an earlier milestone goes first. (2) Within a milestone the item with the cheaper pilot goes
first, because a cheap pilot that fails frees its slot soonest. (3) An item that depends on another waits for it.
A slot is freed when the item passes its gate, when its kill gate fires and the fallback is recorded, or when the
falsifier of its pilot holds.

| Order | Item | Where | Pilot before funding | Kill gate or fallback | Milestone |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Fused per-world step kernel | §21.18 | Fuse kinematics, dynamics terms and one Newton iteration for the reference quadruped; compare with the staged kernels at 8,192 worlds with digests unchanged; one week | KILL-MEGA; the staged step plan | After the M2 profile |
| 2 | Coupled multiphysics islands: free-surface fluid, implicit cloth, Cosserat rod | §19.10.6, §19.10.7 | Proxy adequacy on an incumbent engine: three scenes, 200 episodes each, proxies against full models (GATE-HAB-09) | KILL-LIVING; the Solid tier and the Frontier proxies | After M6; the pilot needs no new solver and may run from M3 |
| 3 | Rotor port for flight, with the aerial-terrestrial hybrid variant | §14.10, §15.4 | Hover by hand: thrust equals weight, ideal induced power T^(3/2)/√(2·ρ·A), and the energy ledger closes | KILL-SINEW-ROTOR; port and variant withdrawn | M6 |
